When we read the committee setting, we can get a different impression. There it is in total, most of the government, everything from municipalities to directorates. I note that it is the opposition that has taken the opportunity to write in most of what they think about general government in general. But it's not a theme, and we, the position, have chosen to stick to what's in the message. It's unfortunate, the way the opposition has done this, because it's going to be a dilution, and we're not getting the attention on this important topic that's been discussed in the message. The majority has developed three themes that we've written a good deal about. It's New Public Management in the public sector, we've written about education and competence, and we've written about financial management, among other things. in relation to the sea. ‘Management’ and ‘bureaucratic’ are often used as shell words, at least in certain parts of the political landscape. In all debates on reduced expenditure, it is the administration to be cut, and that is where there is the opportunity to cut. It is simple, it is non-committal, and, moreover, it is politically opportune. We need a good and loyal administration to implement the political decisions. In its history, administration has been characterised by two governance models. It is the traditional regulatory governance model, and for the last 20 to 30 years, market management principles have also gained greater influence in the public sector. The Chicago School, with Milton Friedman, has given the economic premise to eager politicians from New Zealand to England. In this country, too, it has been politically dominant. These ideas have also been passed on by government, and they are characterised by, among other things. quantitative measurements and fragmentation in small output units to be measured which shall be able to compete with other units. Keywords include outsourcing, privatisation, increased competition and increased use of contractual relationships. The Communication discusses and problematizes these principles as a public sector management system. The majority is clearer in their recommendations. We underline coordination and overall rather than fragmentation and competition, i.e. a way of thinking about governance that directly attacks New Public Management's competitive thinking. We expect, and we expect, that what we write, the notes we put in, and the leaders we put in, will create a new debate within the government and in the government on how to organise the government away from quantitative measurements to develop qualitative parameters if there is a need to use measurement to put numbers or data on efficiency. Management, governance, based on external motivation, as New Public Management operates with, is a bad way to generate the creativity of individual employees. The majority place competence and involvement as alternatives to hierarchical management structures with external motivation as a basis. The best way to create an effective organization is to release people, so don't leave them in the strong hierarchical structures. We want, and we stress, that the public sector should be characterised as innovative, with a focus on education and skills that will stimulate innovation. The public sector shall aim to be the most innovative sector in our society, both in terms of capital and, above all, in terms of human use. The S Sea is a married child of New Public Management, of market liberalism, and we are problematicating the role of the S Sea and its task in the public sector.

Management is therefore challenged in terms of choice between standardised and specialised solutions. At the same time, people have greater expectations for quality, efficiency and service. It is a challenge for ever-growing management. Compared to many other countries, Norway has a large public sector. From the dawn of the welfare state in the 1930s, the Norwegian welfare model has been built on the universality of key welfare schemes. In addition, tasks have traditionally been largely carried out by the public. In the last decade, we've had a larger share of market solutions both nationally and internationally. The Christian People's Party believes that private ideals and voluntary alternatives are important complements to the public in terms of health and care services and e.g. diversity in school. However, the aim must always be diversity and freedom of choice, and not the means in themselves. Governments have undergone major changes in the last 20 years and, as a number of have been involved, they have been more inspired by market solutions. Greater emphasis has been placed on the functioning of economic incentives, the measurement of results, the outsourcing of competition and the elements of reward and penalty systems. Several tasks are now being solved in a market, and this has again created a greater need for supervision and control. This has been justified by the need for increased efficiency and better use of resources. In order to provide a basis for the measurement of results, several public units have also been subdivided into so-called result units. This in turn has led to increased fragmentation and disintegration of management organisations. The Christian People's Party agrees that the intentions have been good enough. The requirement for the public sector has increased. The public is responsible for vital and fundamental services, which mean a lot in people's everyday lives, and which consume a lot of resources. It is reasonable and necessary to focus on the efficient and accurate use of resources. However, many of the key public service objectives, such as: Legal certainty, non-discrimination and reassuring procedures are difficult to measure and deliver. The use of simplified measurement standards may be detrimental to efficiency processes. Management is not, nor shall it be, comparable to normal production. The Christian People's Party therefore believes that public sector governance principles must be interaction and overall over fragmentation and extensive competition. It must be based on competence and target management and not just on measurable formulations. The Christian People's Party wants a strong public sector with a high level of competence, and that private, non-profit and voluntary alternatives become an important complement to the public in terms of welfare services in, among others. health and care.

As several other speakers have pointed out, in several reports, the Riksrevision has for many years revealed widespread violations of public procurement rules. Riksrevisor Jørgen Kosmo says he is ‘in particular, concern that the administration is unable to comply with and practise the public procurement rules. This is worrying, because if the public is unable to comply with its own legislation, there is a great danger that the legislation will be nothing but a good intention.» The left shares the concerns raised by the Riksrevision. The left is also positive that the Soria Moria declaration of raising the tender threshold from the level of DKK 500000 up to the EU level, which in 2005 was around 1.8 million. kr, is now being scraped. On the other hand, there is reason to believe that the red-green government's signal of such a deterioration in the procurement law, combined with the fact that the simplified obligation to publish was dropped, has not stimulated the state and municipality to take the legislation more seriously. The members of the Committee from the opposition, including the Left, indicate that the Government is not nearing the target of 25 pst. public acquisitions shall be made through electronic commerce and the objective shall not be maintained in the notification. Electronic commerce will significantly improve the quality of purchasing practices but will have to lead to a certain conversion of purchasing procedures in many places. The left supports the fact that the government here, like in the area of simplification, goes away from concrete objectives. The absence of objectives weakens the follow-up possibilities. Therefore, the Left believes that the Government should have a concrete objective, concrete measures to achieve the objective and a method of continuous follow-up to the way in which it is conducted. Otherwise, I refer to the proposals that we are with other parties on.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e835	Public procurement policy has been the subject of much debate during this major period. When the red-green government took over after the Bondevik II government, the government had just raised the threshold for demanding a tender from NOK 200000 to NOK 500000. It happened just before the government change. The aim was to simplify everyday life for businesses and for public purchasers. The tender law imposes a number of procedural requirements, which involve costs both for the purchaser and for the seller. The rise in the threshold was the result of a thorough cost-benefit assessment. The disadvantage was that competition could be weakened below the tender threshold, the advantage being that transaction costs would decrease. The Bondevik II Government carefully assessed the benefits and disadvantages and concluded that the tender threshold could be raised to NOK 500000 if, at the same time, a simplified notice obligation was introduced for smaller purchases below NOK 500000. The obligation to publish requires purchasers to provide a brief information on the purchase on the Internet. It was to ensure unbureaucratic compliance with the competition requirement, which also applies under the tender threshold, and that the business community was thus given knowledge of the purchases to be made, rather than that they were made in the form of unsolicited direct purchases. Then the government shifted in the autumn of 2005 and quite a few other tones in its procurement policy, to say the least. The red-green parties entered in the Government Declaration that the tender threshold should be raised to EU level, i.e. from 500 000 kroner up to EU level, which was 1,8 million at the time. kr, so the proposal would have substantially ruined Norwegian procurement policy. In addition, the Grande Røys State Council decided to scrape the simplified notice plan for purchases below NOK 500000. These two interventions were a clear weakening of procurement policy in Norway and gave a clear negative signal to purchasers in both state, county and municipality that follow-up to the legislation was not a priority area for that government. It goes without saying – I think almost most people see this – that when public procurement is made for around 270 billion dollars, it is of great social importance how these purchases are carried out. Purchases are also made against private enterprises, and they are known to be linked together in such a way as to ensure safe jobs, but also to maximise profits for shareholders. This, of course, means that you do not take one penny less than necessary for the goods and services you sell. This raises a number of issues of major societal importance. Do people get the best goods and services for the tax money society uses? Is there a real competition for purchases, so that you know that the best supplier has been chosen and that the price is as low as possible for the given quality? Are purchases made in a clean manner, or are they carried out in a manner that presents a risk of corruption, camaraderie, immaturity and lubrication? Do enterprises – thousands of small and medium-sized enterprises – have a level playing field and have easy knowledge of the purchases to be made? It's unfortunately easy to answer a loud no to all of these four questions. Repeated reports from the National Audit Office leave no doubt: purchases in Norway do not take place under the legislation, and the risk of waste, camaraderie and pre-distribution is high. The same is illustrated by a number of relatively embarrassing revelations related to the ministry’s own purchasing practices, including in offices under the Ministry of Renewal — which should be a matter of considerable concern to the Council of State. The Storting Message The good procurement is made to meet the criticism of the Riksrevision. The right is in favour of a call for tenders, and we are in favour of actions that strengthen competence, which provide more guidance, and which are positive initiatives to enhance the quality of procurement in Norway. However, the right considers that we need stricter regulation, more active follow-up and supervision and greater transparency in public procurement. It is also easy to read the removal of the objective of electronic commerce as clearly defensive. On the other hand, we are delighted that we are going away from the promise to raise the tender threshold, but it was a long step backwards initially. The right is therefore the co-rapporteur for a number of suggestions in the vote: – Simplified publication obligation for purchases between 100 000 and 500000 SEK – Introduction of objectives for electronic commerce – Establishment of supervisory function for public procurement, not just a complaint body – Publication of procurement protocols – Publication of who delivers goods and services to different levels of the public sector These proposals are designed with a certain reservation about investigation, so we ask the Government to evaluate these matters carefully. Acquisition policy has started poorly and has been a neglected area during this large-scale period. The cancellation message is good, but it needs to be stronger in order for the citizens to get the goods and services they should receive, given the resources that are being deployed.

There's no more complicated legislation we need right now. The rules shall ensure that the procurement is open, that it can be verified and that it is conducted on a competitive basis. The rules shall facilitate the detection and prevention of corruption and corruption-like behaviour. There are important measures to ensure transparency of procurement.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e1169	St. pp. No 72 addresses various issues such as road and rail. Of course, I am satisfied that the majority of the committee essentially agrees with the proposals contained in the proposal. In the proposal, the Government proposes that about 17 000 km of the rest of today’s highways be reclassified to the county road, in Oslo municipal road, in connection with the implementation of the administrative reform. The administrative reform is an important reform of the local selfgovernment and will give the county councils a number of new tasks that will strengthen their role as regional development actors. The main consequences of the reform will be seen in the transport sector. Collecting will take over about 17 000 km of the other national road network today. Following the implementation of the reform on 1 January 2010, the county will be responsible for about 44 000 km of long county road network, while the state will be responsible for about 10 000 km on behalf of it. The state road network following the administrative reform will be called national roads and will consist mainly of what we call tribal roads today. The traffic on the roads will be distributed by about 50 pst. on each of the behalf of the parties. In future, municipalities will be an important contributor to national transport policy. The state will play a particularly important role in the national road safety policy. The College of Directors was briefed on 19 May. There is also the law of a full review, even at the level of detail, of this. I understand that the county councils feel that it may be necessary, but as I said, there is no policy in this. There's more technology than politics. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e1695	Today, we are to adopt the Trondheim environmental package. This package will provide the basis for a forward-looking, efficient and environmentally friendly transport system in the Trondheim area. The package is supported by six parties, and thus a large majority in Trondheim City Board. It aims to reduce CO2 emissions by 20 pst. by 2018. The proportion travelling environmentally friendly – walking, cycling and public transport – shall be increased to 50 pst. by 2018. Thus, the share travelling by private car shall be reduced by 50 pst. by the same time. It is regrettable that the Right Party has not been able to participate in this effort for the environment and the climate. It is important that we have brought this matter forward in the spring session of Stortinget, so that Trondheim – and the climate – will thus not wait until October to have it adopted. The proposition states that the State Road Administration believes that it will take about eight months from the decision of the Storting until the recovery can begin. When the Storting now makes its decision here today, it also means that the Government must focus on getting started as quickly as possible with the collection in step 1. The majority believe that the mid-February 2010 deadline should be considered as the last one to put this in place. The county assumes that public transport, operation and investment funds account for more than one fifth of the total environmental package, and that this is also a priority from the start of the period. That view shares the majority of the Committee. In addition to this fifth, Trondheim will also be able to obtain funding from the public transport reward scheme. Together, this will constitute a strong commitment to public transport. It is important to clarify that the means used by means of tolls or fuel charges should be added to existing appropriations. There is now a significant shift in public transport in Trondheim. For example, the phase-in of hybrid buses on line 5 will result in a 40-tonne reduction of CO2 emissions when the bus is at the stop because the engine is not running. The doubling of the reward funds notified in NTP also increases the State co-financing of environmental measures in Trondheim than foreseen. The means of reward are in addition to the incomes of the environmental package and the appropriations from the county. The majority of the Committee considers that the financial shares in operating and investing in public transport should not be reduced, even if the large investment projects included in the package are more expensive than expected. It is also important that the KS1 process focuses on identifying projects that best meet the objectives set out in the environmental package. Climate action must be based on prioritisation between the projects in both step 1 and step 2. The package also mentions a scheme for the collection of local fuel charges to be used for both operation and investment in public transport in addition to road installations. There has been a long wish in this room that there is a law that makes this possible. This is also in line with Government policy, that local authorities need to have more tools in their efforts to reduce climate emissions in the transport sector. This package contains differentiated prices in the payment sections of the package. It is a prerequisite for the country’s financial commitment that the investment scheme should have a traffic-regulating effect. The tariffs therefore have to contribute to better traffic flows, and this will include, inter alia, require higher tariffs during rush time. This is a good day for Trondheim. We're going to have a powerful community commitment that combines road upgrading with climate action and collective action.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e2073	I think it is desirable and necessary for me in this context to say a few words about the situation with regard to influenza A. [[H1N1]] As the Committee’s chairman was also concerned, on 11 June the World Health Organisation, WHO, raised the level of preparedness for phase 6 in the development of the new influenza epidemic. This means that the world has experienced the first flu pandemic since 1968. For the first time in history, however, we are not taken to bed, but, unlike previous pandemics, we have had the opportunity to prepare. The WHO decision to raise the level of preparedness is based on reports from around the world. The latest up-to-date figures from the WHO show that it is so far, per year. 15 June, infected persons are registered in 76 countries with nearly 36 000 confirmed infections, including 163 deaths. Nearly 2,400 infections have been confirmed in the EU and EFTA countries, and the first deaths were reported from Scotland on 14 June. Based on available information, the WHO and Norwegian health authorities also assess the severity of this pandemic to be moderate. The overwhelming majority of infected people have experienced mild symptoms and quickly become completely healthy, often without the need for medical treatment. It's common for viruses to evolve over time. Therefore, the authorities are concerned about getting more knowledge of how this new virus may develop and are prepared to make the picture more serious than it is today. When I attended the World Health Assembly for a while back, it was not least this danger that the virus is not stable and can change, which was stressed very strongly, not least from WHO's Secretary-General, Dr. Chan. In St. prp. No 86 for 2008-2009, Norway has committed itself to buying 9.4 million vaccine doses at an estimated total price of 650 million. kr if the WHO declares phase 6 pandemic further that the new pre-pandemic vaccine agreement will be set aside if the manufacturer starts the production of the pandemic vaccine after the pandemic phase 6 has been declared. With the WHO declaration of phase 6, the premise for proposals for decisions is in St.prp. No 86 for 2008-2009 related to pre-pandemic vaccines, thus changing and triggering the need for an authorisation to order 9,4 million doses of pandemic vaccines within a framework of 650 million. kr. I am pleased that the Stortinget has now taken care of this in the treatment of the cancellation today – that it is likely that the Stortinget can do so. The first delivery of a pandemic vaccine will be available at least three to four months after the start of production, which is expected to take place at the beginning of July. The Institute for Public Health is preparing for the vaccination of the population according to the plans for vaccination by a pandemic. So finally just a few words about the roof stop for doctors, psychologists and physiotherapists. We negotiate with the Norwegian Medical Association, Norwegian Psychological Association and Norwegian Physiotherapy Association on economic conditions, etc. There is now agreement with the Psychological Association and the Physiotherapeutic Association on the conditions for the next period, while the negotiations with the doctors are still ongoing.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e2118	Inner. S. No 296 concerns a proposal by the depositors Gunvald Ludvigsen and Trine Skei Grande on the suspension of the Privacy Commission, the establishment of the core file and the protection of sensitive data. The document promotes a total of seven proposals. Proposal I is the provision of sensitive personal data, so that they are not communicated by health professionals to patients in the presence of unauthorised persons, including a revision of the hospital medical profession. Proposal II concerns the pseudonymisation of individualising characteristics of electronic patient records. In proposal III, the proposers ask the Government to explain the real need for access to postal records across health businesses. Proposal IV applies to patients with a right of access to the patient record. Proposal V concerns the establishment of a moratorium on the establishment of new health records until today’s registers have been reviewed. Proposal VI concerns the establishment of an electronic core file, where individual consents to the storage of vital information is pseudonymised and encrypted. Proposal VII concerns training and awareness-raising measures to ensure respect for professional secrecy. In the Committee's comments, the majority of the Committee, from the Party of Advancement, Right, SV, Christian People's Party and Left, refers to the Commission's quotations. This quote is linked to the Health Research Act adopted on 20 June 2008: ‘The Human Rights Commission has observed that parts of the legislative proposal have been the subject of criticism and discussion by the hearing bodies and the professional community. This applies, inter alia, to the use of so-called broad consents, i.e. consents covering one or more general research purposes and fields of research, without specifying the individual details requested.’;» The majority have an additional quote from the Privacy Commission: ‘Another part of the criticism of the law relates to a weakening of privacy by not fully exploiting the possibilities for de-identification. In the view of the Privacy Commission, a similar criticism can also be addressed to other central health records, such as Norwegian Patient Register, where the legislator has recently shown a tendency to opt out of pseudonymisation as privacy promotion.’ Proposal I, that new procedures be established to ensure that sensitive health information is not communicated in the hearing of others, is supported by an overall committee. A majority consisting of the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the Progress Party are opposed to proposal II, where the Stortinget asks the Government to develop the standard for electronic postal records, so that individualising characteristics of simple operations can be replaced by generated pseudonyms. As far as proposal III is concerned, the majority, all parties minus the Workers' Party and the Centre, believe that the Government should present a large-scale report outlining the real need for access to patient records across the board. Proposal IV, on access to patient logs, has supported all batches during the treatment of Ot.prp. No 51. The proposal has thus been taken care of. Proposal V, on moratorium against new health records, is supported by a minority consisting of Right, SV, Christian People's Party and Left. Proposal VI, establishing an electronic core file, is supported by a minority consisting of Right, SV, Christian People's Party and Left. Proposal VII, the organisation of training and awareness-raising activities to ensure professional secrecy, is supported by the entire Committee. This means, therefore, that on several points the proposers have either received a majority in the Committee or also the entire Committee. Then I want to add to the minute I have left, that I experienced this morning in a discussion on the news channel on TV 2, with the overall responsibility of the National Telemedicine Centre, that the concept of privacy is now clearly given a whole new content. For that person, it argued that privacy also means that the interests of the patient in relation to proper treatment are being taken into account in a different way than in the past. I have also noted that, in correspondence with the Committee on the subject, the Minister for Health has introduced a new concept, namely ‘patient protection’. I see that in that way, with that kind of novelty, a kind of confusion arises about what privacy really is. I would therefore like to quote from Wikipedia in terms of the term: «Personal protection is the protection of the personal individual, but may have a different meaning depending on the context. Two common ways of looking at the concept is to see the concept of a technical and a social context. The technical point of view is to check information about yourself and what it is used for. The social interpretation is about everyone having a need to be alone.’ It is therefore in this context that the majority and the minority in the Committee have stressed the importance of privacy.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e2183	This case testifies to the commitment of the Health and Care Committee to improving patient treatment and privacy to go hand in hand. There are, of course, two joint committee proposals and one of the proposals contained in the proposal for a representative is considered to be taken care of. I want to spend my time commenting on a couple of issues that I think are important in the proposal for a representative. One concerns the moratorium proposed and supported by a minority in the Committee on Health Records. The view expressed in the proposal for a representative, that health records do not save lives, and that none of our health records are used for treatment, surprises me. Last time Left claimed a similar view, the Left’s Storing Group received a letter from the Kreftregistert where the Cancer Register, among others. As a result of their review of the treatment of endometrial cancer and colon cancer in Norway, the number of relapses was reduced from 28 pst. to 9 pst. – approx. 200 people per year were rescued from rectal cancer/endometrial cancer. Similar reviews are carried out in other areas by the Cancer Register, and it bears witness to a somewhat frightening lack of transparency in what is proposed, when you do not get into the letters you receive, and take them into account in your argument. Another relevant issue in relation to this moratorium will be the development of a new national cardiovascular registry. We have discussed here at Stortinget how the treatment of stroke patients is very different in Norway – 15 p.m. receive thrombolysis, i.e. blood-closing medical treatment, in the Haukeland business area, while on average only 1 pst. receive it on a national basis. The hospital in Fredrikstad provides more such advanced treatment, much more than the Ullevål University Hospital. has been able to do so in the same time period since it was introduced, so there are major differences. This applies to 15,000 patients a year — only the stroke patients, and many others have different cardiovascular diseases. It's obvious that such a health record in the cardiovascular area, if we got the same quality of it as the Cancer Register, would mean a lot to save lives and get the opportunity to treat these patients better. So it's alarming, I think, that a minority can jump on a proposal on moratorium in this way. I would like to say that other health records and quality records that you work with in the professional environments, for example. in the context of neurological diseases such as Alzheimer’s, ALS, MS, Parkinson’s, it will also be very important to look at this on the Stortinget, in order to improve the quality of such vulnerable disease areas as this. I think that the majority refer to in the vote, namely that the Council of State and the Ministry have set up a national project, which started in 2008, to ensure quality and privacy in health records, is a much better way to go. Rapid conclusions on these demanding issues should not be promoted. This also applies to national nuclear records. A single committee is for a national core file, but the majority does not agree to conclude now that it should be consent-based, pseudonymised, encrypted, etc. We think we need an investigation of that, an overview of what, among other things. national core file shall be included. Also, the proposal, which one jumps on well quickly, about the pseudonymisation of e-mails, which the minority promote, seems a little bit low-quality. When asked how the Ministry of Health views that proposal, the Ministry of Health responds in a letter to the Committee that it does not seem to them to make sense, because there are rules for exemption from public disclosure, rules for non-public mail to be derived from e-mail lists, etc., and you do not understand how encryption or pseudonymisation should work in relation to it for the e-mail system. There is nothing said by the proposers about how you think it is, and there is very little said in the attitude of the minority. So I want to cooperate consistently on better privacy, but I believe that we need to work more closely with the proposals than what happens in the vote by the minority.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e2282	It is essential for healthcare that it is able to ensure safe and secure communication of health information. This applies both to verbal communication and to written and electronic communication. Health information shall be treated with caution and respect for the integrity of the information to which it relates. In case of hospitalisation, the information may be divulged by the doctor by other patients or perhaps also by visitors to the hospital to overhear a conversation between a doctor and his or her patient. I agree, of course, with the Committee that this must be avoided as far as possible. State health surveillance may require the correction of the circumstances in which establishments are operated in a manner which may have harmful consequences for patients or otherwise be unlucky or irresponsible, including the observance of the obligation of professional secrecy. I would like to ask the Health Directorate and the Health Inspectorate to assess what measures the Directorate and supervision consider necessary to ensure better privacy in the treatment and contact of patients. With regard to the use of pseudonyms in an electronic post-log, I am uncertain whether this will lead to a strengthening of privacy. In any case, those who carry and receive the mail will have to know the identity of the consignor or consignee for replying to the enquiry. It must always be checked that information posted on an electronic postal record record does not contain any information related to confidentiality. Even if you were to change the standard of an electronic postal record, you would still be at risk of human error. It is also the Ministry of Culture and the Church that is responsible for archival law. The need for access to patient information across healthcare companies was discussed during the debate on Ot. pp. No 51 on 10 June. The proposition is based, inter alia, on: on the report of the regional health enterprises to the Ministry of Health in December 2006, where the need for specialist health care has been addressed. The Norwegian Center for Electronic Patient Records conducted an investigation for the Health Directorate in 2007 about the same thing, and I'm content to refer to it. As Minister of Health and Care, I am committed to ensuring good privacy. It is impossible to ensure good and sound health care without, at the same time, ensuring good data protection. A central element of data protection is the right and effective ability of individuals to have control of health information about themselves. I am therefore pleased that a majority in Odelstinget joined the position of the Health and Care Committee to legislate the patient's right of access to the log. Technological development is increasingly providing better and new opportunities for obtaining health information for use in treatment and research, but it also poses major data protection challenges. The use of technologies to improve privacy and provide good regulation will provide the basis for continuing to collect and use data for, inter alia: research. The central health and quality records shall be reviewed with a view to better exploitation, better quality and safer handling of research and health monitoring data. New health records may be proposed if considered important for a better health care system. The Ministry has a central health register under investigation, often referred to as the core journal. Healthcare professionals shall be able to access critical information on individual patients in case of unplanned health care needs. A prerequisite for providing rapid, effective professional and sound health care to a patient is to know who this patient is. Health records used for patient treatment must have health records which are recognisable on the basis of a person’s identity. Therefore, information contained in a core file cannot be stored pseudonymised. Encryption will be assessed, but it is too early to conclude whether this is possible and appropriate. Knowledge and attitudes of health care providers are important. The management of any health activity is responsible for providing the necessary training, knowledge and attitudes, so that employees are well aware of the rules on information security, including professional secrecy, patient rights, procedures, etc. This will be included in the information security regulation currently under work. I will also consider asking the Health Directorate to develop a supervisor for this work.

There is a register of neurological diseases — treatment of Parkinson’s disease, ALS, Alzheimer’s disease and MS — where there is a great need to see whether we have sufficient quality of treatment in Norway, in order to get a better overview of how these patients are treated, so that we can make the improvements that the Stortinget definitely wants. The third thing I mentioned was the treatment of patients with muscle and skeletal disorders. So I haven't mentioned an infinite list. I have mentioned some specific areas with very large patient groups. On the issue of how to ensure the protection of privacy and the quality of these registers, the Council of State launched a project in 2008 to review the quality, privacy and practices of the different patient registries. When Stortingen is going to process new registers, we're going to have to take a stand on what kind of legislation we're going to have in the areas that are listed here today, in the individual case. It is clear that it is important to look at why, for example: The Register of Cancer has been able to obtain the results that the representative Ludvigsen will not deal with. When he says in his proposal that the Cancer Registry does not save lives, that it is not used for medical treatment, I refer to the review of patients with colon cancer and endometrial cancer, where an estimated 200 more patients are rescued this year because of the Cancer Register, which has been person-identifiable, so that they have been able to go through the course of treatment. You don't relate to that kind of argument. I would like us to do the same, and that we could work more together on these issues – better patient treatment, better privacy. It has been said that we do not hear hearings, etc. Both privacy in relation to actively informed consent and the legalisation of this with the patient’s access to the log were two important reinforcements that were decided on here in the Stortingen last week, and that included, among others. based on referrals from the Health Authority. We shall listen to each other in this debate. I wish we could avoid black paint and try to take care of both patient care and privacy.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e2521	I noticed the discussion between Gunnvald Ludvigsen and Jan Bøhler about the creation of new health records. I tried to think, what of the body is it that these don't cover? If you had created a register for inmates as well, you would have covered everything. I think that where Gunvald Ludvigsen pointed this out in relation to person-identifiable records, Jan Bøhler's answer makes it obvious that it almost just becomes the imagination that sets limits on what kind of new registers to be created. I registered during the hearing that Camilla Stoltenberg suggested that a vaccine registry be used, because that would be practical. Then you would be able to register the entire population in such a register with respect to when it was given the vaccine, and who had received the vaccine. On the question of whether it should not be after active consent, she argued that it would be impractical because the register would not be as complete as desired. If you then start to look at the Health Register Act of 2001, both it and the pre-workers of the Health Register Act – with a public investigation back in the 1990s – make it clear that already at that time it was completely unnecessary to set up person-identifiable health registers for research. It is therefore fully possible for the researcher to follow events over time — including the individual patient — and yet not to identify the individual. In my view, therefore, Gunvald Ludvigsen's approach is entirely plausible, even in the case of cancer records. It would have been quite possible to pseudonymize cancer records and yet save lives, which Jan Bøhler is concerned about. There is nothing technically that would prevent it, and nothing technically prevented it ten years ago either. Therefore, it is quite amazing that the majority will adopt a law, as it did when it applied to Ot.prp. No 51 for 2008-2009, on solutions which are technically not feasible at all — while it will not be possible to pass on the law adopted under a unanimous decision on solutions that are technically feasible. I think that is once again supported by the Council of State, when he implements the concept of 'patient protection'. I don't know about the term "patient protection" yet. This morning, the head of the National Telemedicine Centre deliberately used the term ‘personal protection’ to ensure treatment for the individual patient and thus protect it. With that kind of confusion, it is also unclear what you are discussing in relation to privacy. That is why I am a strong supporter of precise conceptual usage. In connection with the Health Register Act, precise terms were adopted, but I see that a majority in this room is no longer particularly concerned with precise concepts. The same applies in relation to the concept of ‘core journal’. The core journal can be defined precisely, but I also see that the Council of State is using vague terms as to what is in the term. To be clear on where you want to go in this case, I think it is appropriate to be precise in terms of use.

The escalation plan for the drug field was presented in the autumn of 2007. The escalation plan provides the framework for government policy in this area. The initiatives in the drug field must be seen in the context of, inter alia: efforts to prevent poverty and efforts in housing policy and in the field of mental health. The plan has 147 interventions in the prevention, treatment, rehabilitation, research and building of competences in the field of drugs. Most of the measures in the escalation plan for the drug field are in progress, 74 pst. according to the Health Directorate’s annual status report for this plan. Many of the measures are evaluated independently, and I will provide an overall review of the experience of the plan. At the same time, it is important to include experiences from other countries’ drug policies. The escalation plan needs to be replaced by a new, long-term strategy that takes a holistic view of the challenges in the field. In order to achieve a good strategy, I will focus on a thorough process of broad involvement of all stakeholders, both users, relatives, volunteers, lawmakers, health companies, municipalities, private actors and, of course, the Stortinget. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3023	Drug use and drugrelated crime are one of the biggest and most serious problems in today’s society. It is very complex, because it is both a medical, social and, to some extent, a criminal problem. In recent years, a practice has developed to accept the use and possession of drugs. Some places are sold and used openly. This is why it is time for us to take a clear approach to drug policy. Right will have zero tolerance on drugs. The number of addicts no longer increases as much as in the past, and the number of overdose deaths has decreased in the last three years. It is nevertheless very tragic for those affected. The new challenge is that more addicts who die from an overdose have never been in contact with the treatment device. Young and often socially well-adapted young people are starting their drug abuse as an alternative to alcohol use. The drugs problem concerns us all. They hit many people, families and their surroundings. In addition to the many human tragedies and the social and health consequences of the abuse, the problems create insecurity in everyday life for most people. It is important to maintain the long-term objective of drug policy: a society free from abuse. The failure of everything in the fight against drugs must not lead us to abandon this objective. A civilised and humane society cannot allow human beings to go down without trying to prevent it. Some claim that experimentation with drugs is the result of our freedom of choice. But when addiction to drugs is there, there is no longer any free choice, either for dependent parents or siblings or for those with parents who are drug addicts. A society shall facilitate our development and personal freedom for development and growth. However, it is necessary for a democratic society to limit and combat what violates the freedom of individuals and the environment. Everyone has the right to a worthy life. So society has to set some limits. It is important to have knowledge of what is happening in the different youth cultures. Youth is seeking identity and community, and it will often be central to the choices they make. Young people do not just participate in their own environments. Identity and belonging are built on the arenas in which we all participate. It is therefore a shared responsibility to form countercultures, to build up attitudes towards drugs and to hinder the availability of illicit drugs. Significant human and financial resources are being invested in prevention today. A key tool is to take care of and strengthen the drugfree environment. Prevention of drug abuse is also about creating good growth conditions for children and young people. If we are to be able to succeed in the prevention work, cooperation between the various departments needs to be strengthened and the various measures need to be better coordinated. As parents and engaged citizens, the idea is that our children are going to start experimenting with drugs a little of what we fear the most. I don't want 14–15-year-olds to know that there are places where they can easily buy drugs. I don't want the schoolyard to be a drug marketplace. I want young people to experience that adults are setting limits on drugs. We're going to care about those who are addicted to drugs, and help to ensure better quality of life. Those with drug problems should be given help for treatment and weaning and opportunities for a new chance of a drug-free life. For this reason, the Right has contributed to providing drug-dependent patient rights and to escalating drug treatment appropriations significantly in its alternative budgets, including during the period when we were in the Cooperative Government. We have also increased the number of treatment sites for medically assisted treatment. It is also important to ensure good post-employment protection, to ensure that they are given employment measures and opportunities, as well as good housing opportunities. But the most important thing is to prevent the recruitment of young people into the drug environment. I'm not picking up any injection rooms right now. We dealt with that the other day, so I don't want to get into it. But I would also like to praise the Christian People's Party for addressing this issue and for talking a lot about the prevention of drug abuse. I think that's important. Let me state that the Government maintains the vision of a society free from drug abuse. The overall objective of Norwegian drug policy is to reduce the negative impacts of drug use on individuals and society. Drug trafficking is and shall be prohibited. Norway shall promote a restrictive drug policy which, at the same time, maintains a fundamental humane approach, which takes care of the weakest and which is enshrined in UN human rights law. The needle and syringe exchange scheme is not intended to replace prevention and rehabilitation measures, but to complement a comprehensive response chain for those with major health problems and major difficulties in getting out of their drug dependence. The escalating plan for the drug field runs – as has been said for several rounds here this evening – until 2010. At the same time, it is important to include experience from other countries’ policies. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3388	After almost four hours of drug and alcohol debate, which is very engaging, it is now time to talk about rehabilitation. This is the subject of this proposal. The proposers argue that the purpose of habilitation and rehabilitation is to give people affected by injury, disease or disability a new opportunity to master everyday life and to participate in social life and work, while reducing the need for healthcare and social security services. Furthermore, several studies indicate that there is a need for much better provision both at municipal level and in the specialist health service. The proposers therefore believe that it is alarming when the number of days of treatment in training institutions has been reduced by nearly 54 000 under the government of Stoltenberg, while the need for rehabilitation is actually increasing. A Health and Welfare Committee as a whole expressed its opinion in its Budgetary configuration S. No. 11 for 2006–2007 that the next major health policy area should be habilitation and rehabilitation. This considers that the proposers must take the form of an obligatory stepping-up plan with earmarked means which ensure better capacity and quality of the offer. The majority of the Committee – The Advance Party, the Right, the Christian People’s Party and the Left – point out that on many occasions the professional communities and patient organisations have expressed themselves critical of the government’s lack of commitment in this important area, and believe that the expectations of the ‘National Strategy for Habilitation and Rehabilitation 2008–2011’ have gradually changed to great frustration and disappointment. When more and more people fail to fulfil their rights and the need is increasing, it shows that the cross-policy objective of offering it to all those who need it is not being achieved. In order to increase research and technical development efforts, the Committee will channel funds for research and development of competences both to the primary health service and to the specialist health service, but also to private and private/ideal actors in this field. In the report on nationwide supervision of child habilitation services, in 2006, the Health Inspectorate revealed significant aberrations and weaknesses in four out of five municipalities. There are also large geographical differences in treatment provision. The Committee therefore considers that the organisation and financing of these services need to be modified in order to ensure the equivalence of offers. The Committee is committed to maximising the use of all rehabilitation capacity that meets the applicable quality requirements, whether public, private or private, and to adapting quality and quantity to the needs of users. A majority of the opposition parties consider it critical that training institutions throughout the country still operate with reduced capacity, and that many actors have had to put down whole or part of the offer at a time when more and more patients are lacking an offer. The Committee also points out that diabetes and COLS are areas of action with their own plans, but the opposition parties point out that the government has only slightly followed up the plans financially. The growing need is not met sufficiently by fresh means and by increasing the number of places but by deprioritising other groups. The Committee is aware that more patients now have to pay for the rehabilitation work themselves, which helps to reinforce social health differences. The opposition parties also address the need to increase the number of treatment trips abroad, and will allow patients with a special need for escorts and, where required by the treatment institution, to have a companion with them. The opposition is of the opinion that private and private non-profit institutions cannot be regarded as complementary, but must be regarded as part of the ordinary public treatment offer, and sees it as unfortunate that health enterprises have a double role as buyers and performers of the same services. They also believe that a new financial instrument must be put in place. Today’s funding system leads to different practices and varying follow-up and offers in municipalities, and the opposition parties therefore believe that rehabilitation should be funded by the State. The opposition suggests that the government’s arrangements for the municipal sector are now leading to a worse economy and a lower risk of an even worse rehabilitation offer, while the members of the government parties consider the opposite. Now it's the way that good suggestions can be repeated, but there's nothing wrong with us deleting point 9, so we don't have to vote over it.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3491	The issue of the size of the national strategy for habilitation and rehabilitation in 2008–2011 is addressed in the context of the 2008 State budget. I intend to draw up a status paper halfway through the strategy period, which will form the basis for the Ministry of Health and Welfare priorities for the last part of the strategy period. This status document will be presented in the 2010 draft budget. Habilitation and rehabilitation are an important field in which this government focuses. Rehabilitation is a broad and comprehensive field, where multi-storey services are necessary to achieve a good and comprehensive rehabilitation process. In healthcare, habilitation and rehabilitation take place both in municipalities and in specialist health services. In the area of specialist health care, rehabilitation services are provided in hospitals, both in physical medicine and rehabilitation departments and in other departments, in outpatient clinics, through rehabilitation teams and through learning and mastering centres. Special hospitals are available for rehabilitation, and private training and rehabilitation facilities are provided. Following the transfer of responsibility for services in private training and rehabilitation institutions to regional health establishments, this offer has been made in conjunction with other specialist health services. Contracts require the provision of services. This means that quality is better than in the past. The number of doctors, physiotherapists, ergot therapists, nurses and sports educators at the institutions has also improved. According to statistics from the Norwegian Patient Register, there were 33,571 rehabilitation stays in 2008. In 2005, there were 32,920 stays. The number of stays decreased slightly in 2006 — the first year after the transfer — but has increased in both 2007 and 2008. Now it is assessed more individually how long a stay should be and which patients should have resided in an institution. Some institutions offer day-care services. Some of the patients who had previously stayed in private training or rehabilitation institutions receive, and should receive, services in the municipality. I agree that it is fully possible to do more in this area. That's why we also have a national strategy to build this field. It is under construction, both in terms of competence and capacity. The interaction reform will have a clear focus on rehabilitation, and it is communicated from me earlier, also here in the Chamber. So the fact that rehabilitation will be part of the interaction reform is important. But the main focus there is going to be on municipal rehabilitation, not because we are going to transfer tasks from the specialist health service to the community health service – the specialist health service is also to be extended in this area – but I think the biggest failure in the rehabilitation field is at the municipal level. Therefore, the interaction reform will focus in particular on the municipal field, and then the status report I say will be presented in the context of the state budget, in particular focusing on the further development of the specialist healthcare service. It is important to say that a working group set up by the Health Directorate has carried out an overall review of the framework conditions of private enterprises in the area of rehabilitation following the national strategy for rehabilitation and rehabilitation. The Health Directorate has issued a report which is now being discussed in the Ministry of Health and Welfare. It's also part of the summary we have to do halfway through the strategy period. Finally, I just want to say that it is entirely possible to discuss greater needs in the rehabilitation field. That is precisely why we are working on expanding it, and that is exactly why the area of rehabilitation will also be an important part of the interaction reform.

The average duration of treatment for neck injuries in insurance cases in Norway is nine years. For more than 15 years, we have regularly read about unworthy treatment and even about people who, because of lack of follow-up and pain, have taken their own lives. The lack of diagnosis, and the failure to take ISO-certified accompanying images in insurance cases, indicate that the patient is being deprived of rights both in the healthcare system, in the Nav and in other treatment services. The system error itself occurs at the moment you are categorised as an insurance case. The WHO guidelines disappear from the health care system, the Nav budget and the insurance component disappear as dew for the sun and are replaced by the WAD system, which has been developed in such a way as to safeguard the interests of insurance operators rather than the needs of patients. Since I am a little proud of those who sit in the committee with me, I would like to end by quoting Guro Birkeland, doctor, lawyer and secretary-general of the Norwegian Patient Association: ‘If the patient was in the centre, the Norwegian healthcare system would look quite different.’ This proposal puts the patient in the centre. I am promoting this proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3867	I will try to give the best possible answer. The fact that it says ‘indemnities, law and insurance’ has something to do with Norway’s implementation of a system called WAD. It is classified in four sections — the most serious cases of neck injury are categorised as WAD III and WAD IV. If you get that diagnosis, you end up on the outside of the World Health Organisation's guidelines. The WHO ICD-10 codes and the ICF coding system are set aside in the Norwegian system, and you only relate to the WAD system, i.e. circumvention of the whole system developed by the World Health Organisation as guidelines for the treatment of neck injuries and ratified by Norway. That's why it's very important that the directorate as such do a job and get it implemented in place when it comes to damage, law and insurance, because that's where the big mistake in today's system lies, and that's where we don't meet our international commitments to the World Health Organisation. So in many ways, if you take away what goes on in terms of damage, law and insurance, you get a situation where you stand on the ground resting. So I want to say that if you want a change in the system that is there today, you have to go to the Right, Christian People's Party and Left for the proposal as it exists.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3920	: In Document No 8:91. Members of the Advance Party propose to include Capio Anorexia Center AS in the free choice of hospitals. With regard to the theme of eating disorders, there is a cross-political consensus that it is important to get good deals. Everyone agrees with the description given in the proposal, that Capio’s offer is good, and that we need a wide range of treatment services for eating disorders. There are several forms of treatment when a person is struggling with eating disorders, and we have received a lot of input during the treatment of the case, and many have shared their experiences and concerns with us. We're all touched by it, and we see the problems that exist. I think I have the whole committee with me when I say that the offer given around, is not good enough, does not meet everyone who needs it, and that it must be closely followed up by us as health politicians. Capio Anorexia Centers in Fredrikstad offers good treatment, and many want the offer. The problem, as outlined in the proposal, is that Capio does not come under the free choice of hospitals, and therefore cannot be chosen by those who want it. Capio applied for approval both in 2006 and 2008 for hospital status, but was refused in line with the specialist health service law §4-1. One of the reasons for the refusal is that free hospital choice is a right for the patient, and not a scheme in which the individual institution can apply for participation. Capio is not approved as a hospital, so they fall outside. Capio applied for hospital approval, which they have not received, on the basis that the requirements for what a hospital should contain are not met. The majority of the Committee, consisting of the Labour Party, the Right, the SV, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the Left, point to the establishment of a national clinical network for the treatment of eating disorders and the professional capacity of all outpatient clinics to treat and follow up persons with this diagnosis. Treatment responsibility should be in healthcare companies, and since the offer is now being built up there, the consequence is that fewer places are purchased by Capio than before. It is mainly Health Nord that has bought places recently, but also here a regional centre will be established, and so these places will also fall away. Capio, like several other good health institutions, does not fall into the free hospital selection scheme because it is not a hospital. This is well established in the proposal, which clarifies that such a link would require legislative change. However, it is possible to agree on the purchase of services, which the proponents of the Progress Party believe is not enough. Another majority, from the Party of Advancement, the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left, point out that the waiting time is too long and problematic for those who struggle. They therefore want a change in the law. It is clarified by the majority of the Committee, the Labour Party, the Right, the SV, the Christian People's Party and the Center Party, that now the Council of State must take responsibility and give clear notice in the mission document that the Capio offer will be used more. It is important for us that the diversity of the offer is used to achieve as much as possible and that offers that work should live. This is what we expect to be followed up. The Progress Party, which is proposing together with the Right, refers to what is happening on Capio, and proposes this amendment, and I expect them to argue for and substantiate their own proposals. Capio provides health services that I think all the committee members see we need. The question then is: what is a hospital, and when can institutions come under free hospital choice? There are several institutions that want to become part of the scheme, and it may mean a new view of what a hospital should be, whether there are still criteria for one to be approved. I take up the minority proposal and rather come back with our own argument afterwards.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3949	This matter is being followed today by a lot of people with eating disorders and a lot of relatives. Anorexia is a serious mental disorder, and many young people are affected by this disease in the most important part of their lives, which is of great importance in terms of education, working in the workplace and starting up a family. Many young people, especially girls, but also boys, get the disease, and the numbers are big. Public health services have little been able to provide a good treatment offer, and many are getting tired early and suffer from lifelong suffering. Anorexia is also the mental disorder with the highest mortality. Patients with anorexia and eating disorders are a patient group entitled to a treatment offer adapted to their condition. As the Progress Party sees it, this group has not received what they are entitled to. We also believe that this patient group, in line with other patients, should have freedom of choice in terms of treatment provision. This option must also include private, approved treatment centres such as Capio, because it does not help patients to choose between different offers when the offers do not work or in the worst case makes the patient sicker. Patients often wait years before they get the help they need, and unfortunately, many people never get help. I refer to the many personal letters and to one that came today, where he tells how she feels they are being met in the public health system: «What we met was ignorance, unwillingness, lack of resources and laaaaaang waiting time». Lack of knowledge in many cases leads to patients getting sicker, and it can have serious consequences. Patients are often placed with other mentally ill patients with completely different disorders, and they are then treated. Patients do not recover when they land in the wrong place and are subjected to mistreatment. A recently submitted SINTEF report showed that over 30 pst. mental illness was mistreated and misplaced, and I guess the group we're talking about here today is within that group. What is it that makes patients and relatives call and fight to get treatment at a private treatment centre like Capio? Well, the patients meet with respect and dignity, the staff have acute competence, they get individual treatment and close follow-up, the feeling of a home and not an institution. They are met with patience, they get the test and the wrong, they get a long-term plan, and there are exit apartments where they try to live and make their dinner on their own. It is cooperation with the home and home community, and good results are shown. There are also plenty of results from the treatment period with the same concept in Sweden, where Norwegian patients travelled earlier. There is something wrong when the patient is able to choose treatment in a psychiatric department called hospital but is not allowed to choose treatment at the same level at an institution where the so-called hospital definition is not met. The Health and Welfare Committee has entered the budget at the beginning of the year. S. No 11 for 2008–2009 stated that Capio should be an offer for patients across the country. In Dagsnytt 18 on 25 February, Ballo promised on behalf of the Government that, if necessary, the offer would be made available under free hospital choice. But patients are still waiting. The council saw, when I raised the question in the questionnaire, no reason to follow up the case, and Capio still has a lot of available capacity and no predictability as a result of their not being under free hospital choice. The question now is how long the centre is able to operate without a predictable patient base. The descent has already begun a long time ago. This is not about common sense or economics, because the private offer costs no more than the public offering. This is about politics, and in this case the red-greens have met themselves in the door. It is not possible to maintain private treatment sites that do not obtain patients or long-term contracts. It will not be possible for patients to choose Capio as a treatment site as long as it is up to healthcare companies to decide. I think that's the story. This issue is a good example of the red-green health policy, a policy that allows the ideology that the public knows and can best come before the patients' needs. The eagerness to discriminate between private and public and out-of-state private has shown here what it leads to. I had a little hope that SV would support the proposal, especially after Ballos' clear promises, but this only shows that ideology, control and politics are ahead of common sense and patient rights, and it will be a great disappointment today if the two proposals we are now promoting do not get a majority.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e3977	There is a broad consensus that patients with eating disorders are not receiving adequate public health care assistance today. There has been a positive development in this area, but the offer is still incomplete. According to the free choice.net website, the waiting time for daily treatment for children and adolescents with eating disorders is between 3 and 14 weeks, depending on the place of treatment. For adults with eating disorders, waiting time is up to 20 weeks for daily treatment by some health care providers. Patients who require daily treatment for their eating disorders are seriously ill. When we are seriously ill, it is unsustainable to wait for many weeks for help. The right-hand health fraction has received several enquiries from desperate people with eating disorders and their relatives. They tell us about serious health problems and inadequate follow-up from healthcare. Several have pointed out that they have received very good help at Capio. Some have had to pay for this, including: by means of family loans. Although Capio has a professionally recognised offer with which patients are satisfied, many patients feel that they are not allowed to make use of this offer for the benefit of the public. But the most eye-catching thing is that Capio Anorexia Centers are among the offers that the government Stoltenberg wants to remove. Both the Soria Moria Declaration and the Regional Health Companies mission document clearly indicate that private, commercial offers should be limited. The justification seems to be a purely ideological defence of the public service monopoly. When we register a certain attendance from the government parties in this matter, there is clear evidence that this monopoly policy does not face reality. A policy where good private offerings are only available to those who have good advice is not in contact with individuals, especially when the health queue has increased by 65,000 patients under this government. The right considers that private services contribute to the quality and diversity of public health services. Many private actors have been pioneers in professional development and good resource use. The private provision improves public health and increases patients’ freedom of choice. These offers should not be reserved for patients with a good economy who can buy out of an ever-increasing health queue. We succeeded in this policy during the Cooperation Government, when the health queue and waiting time were significantly reduced. In fact, in some healthcare companies, all patients experience a breach of the assessment guarantee. Children and young people entitled to mental health protection experience 18 pst. that they are not treated within the medical deadline, while the proportion is 15 pst. among adults. It is clearly contrary to the intentions of the legislation, but it is actually a good piece of work as it is being done today. We clearly believe that today's contractual system leads to a significant restriction on access to services, and if you want to put patients' interests at the centre, all good arguments are made that today's contractual system should be removed. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4151	It is very important that there is good access to mental health protection, and the Committee agrees that it is not good enough throughout the country, despite the fact that capacity has increased significantly through the mental health and mental health escalation plan. Regional health businesses are responsible for providing the population with the necessary health study in a medical specialist. The services are financed by operating grants from health enterprises, price-fixing payments from the population, and the prostheses from the patients. If patients want a publicly funded treatment offer from private operators, there must be an agreement between the regional health establishments and the specialist or institution concerned. According to the Local Health Act Chapter 4, if the municipality or social security is to cover the treatment, there is a requirement to have an agreement between the municipality and the private health service. The majority of the Committee considers that the requirement for agreements with regional health establishments or municipalities as a condition for reimbursement is necessary in view of the overall use of healthcare resources. It is about how this should be governed – every day and in every area. Then there must be some agreements that ensure good governance with the political priorities and health priorities that are to be done every day in all municipalities in the country. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4168	Evaluation of the Mental Health Progression Plan shows major weaknesses in most areas, in which wages were also involved. The lack of professional staff is high, and patients wait for months and years at an hour with a psychologist or psychiatrist – if they get at all a few hours. This problem is not unknown, but it has not been a political will to increase the availability of publicly paid services. John Alvheim was a predictable man, he got right. Two years ago, I asked the then Health Minister, Brustad, if she would consider re-establishing the system for non-contractual psychologists and psychiatrists to receive reimbursement from popular security, so that the patient can choose who they are going to go to, and so that the need for more people in these professions can be regulated in line with what patients actually need. At the time, there was a notice in the evening post saying that patients were calling about to fifty different psychologists and there were waiting hours in well over a year. She also said that questions related to reimbursement were one of the things they were going to go through at the time, and then they – I expect it to be the government – would conclude when that job was done. Clearly, the conclusion must have been that it is more important to limit publicly paid treatment hours than to meet the patient’s need for treatment, as reflected in the National Audit Office’s report, where it appears that there is often availability within the individual healthcare provider and not the patient’s condition that determines whether the patient is granted treatment and what treatment is offered. This is serious. Now a new scheme within Health South-East is being tried to help patients to order an hour.
