The greatest increase in disability rates occurs among young men, and the cause is more and more frequent mental illness. Waiting times, lack of availability and curtailed offers help make patients sicker. These are patients who need individual treatment, and where the patient's trust in the therapist or doctor is very important. This means that freedom of choice is important, and that the ability to change treatment must also be real. The increase in the number of psychologists as a result of the escalation plan was only 60 pst. of what was the original objective. A lot of psychologists and psychiatrists are missing. The upgrading in the municipalities and within the DPS is not sufficiently close to the tasks they have to perform. By opening up all authorised psychologists and psychiatrists to offer their services with reimbursement rights to patients referred to treatment — and on the basis of what wages also stated — shorter waiting times and far greater options will be provided than we have today. This will help to ensure equivalent services to the weakest and help reverse the major health inequalities created in recent years. It will also inspire more people to train and specialize in mental health protection and lead to better quality and availability, which will benefit the entire community in the next row. The Progress Party believes in better health provision by providing short waiting times, greater accessibility, better quality and freedom of choice. We believe it is important for everyone to be able to use the professional skills that actually exist in the country today, paid with public reimbursement for the benefit of the patients.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4186	The proposers consider it appropriate to provide for a system whereby authorised psychologists and psychiatrists are granted the right of establishment and reimbursement. The proposal does not require an operational agreement with healthcare providers for psychologists and psychiatrists in order to be able to practise with social security suspension. The government’s overall health care objective is that everyone should have equivalent access to healthcare and good health care when they need it. In this context, I would like to refer to a new waiting guarantee for children and young people under the age of 23 with mental and drug-related disorders. No person with the right to the necessary health care shall wait for treatment in the specialist health care for more than 65 working days and all shall be entitled to assessment within 10 working days. The latest figures from the Norwegian patient registry show that a total of 90 pst. in patients under 23 years of age with psychiatric disorders and drugrelated disorders, the referral was evaluated within 10 working days. In the 2009 mission paper, I have called for regional health companies to set up systems to coordinate referrals to the specialist healthcare services in the field of mental health protection, so that patients do not turn to more service providers for help. This requirement applies in particular to references to contract specialists. The system of operating contracts and operating grants was introduced by the Local Health Service Act of 1984. The purpose of the establishment of operational agreements was to relate to the responsibilities of municipalities and counties — later regional health enterprises — to provide health services available to the population. In order to ensure that regional health undertakings are able to discharge their responsibilities, they may either provide services in their own health establishments or enter into contracts with private individuals. In other words, regional health enterprises must have a right of access to the resources related to their population responsibilities. Private practices were still possible on social security account, without an operating contract even after 1984. This led to a distorted geographical distribution of the offer and a concentration of doctors, physiotherapists and psychologists without an operational agreement in and around the major cities. In particular, on the basis of this distribution of bias, the right to reimbursement of doctors, physiotherapists and specialists in clinical psychology who had not agreed with the municipality or county council was terminated from 1993. The scope of treatment from established practices continued to increase even after new non-contractual practices were no longer established financed by social security. This led to significant public funds being channelled into healthcare services without planned arrangements against priority patient groups and good geographical distribution. As of 1998, the right to reimbursement of medical specialists and specialists who did not have an operating agreement with the county councils was therefore completely terminated. Some of the funds released were earmarked for the establishment of new operating agreements in the county and resulted in a sharp increase in the number of years of operation of private contract specialists. Since 1998, there has been only a small increase in agreed annual works. Against this background, and in order to ensure better resource use, the National Health Plan announced that work would be undertaken to change the framework conditions in order to improve the integration of contract specialists into regional health care companies' responsibilities and to continue the private-practice contract specialists scheme. The Ministry will, within a short period of time, submit a proposal for a new framework for contract specialists. The proposal involves bringing together the contractual responsibilities of the various agreements with regional health establishments. The established tariff systems for contract specialists will initially be continued as a financing system. I believe that such a change will provide an important basis for integrating the contract specialists into caring for responsibility in a better way than today. In my opinion, free reimbursement rights for psychologists and psychiatrists will not facilitate such integration, nor will they facilitate a good and fair use of resources.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4373	This is one of the issues in which the Committee's work shows that it is possible to agree on the main objectives. The main goal here is very clear. It is to improve both the knowledge of and the offer of children with autism. The proposers have tabled six proposals in their Document No. Eight proposals. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4382	International studies show that 70 out of 10,000 people can have autism spectra diagnostics, ASD. In Norway, this means that about 20 000 boys may have the diagnosis within the autism spectrum. So this is a large group of people with a disease that is still too small to know about, both in terms of cause and treatment and the underlying medical conditions. This is a group of patients that is different but often requires close follow-up both by health professionals and by their own family. There are also many cases of coercive use, and many difficult situations may arise in everyday life. It turns out that by providing a more comprehensive treatment and by making some changes in the diet and other, the health status and everyday life can be much better. That is why, as the Progress Party sees it, there is good reason to invest resources in research, increased competence, extended diagnosis and the organisation of alternative and more holistic approaches to treating the disease. From the public perspective, different variants of behavioural analytical training are offered, but, as we are familiar with, the provision of medical investigations and related treatment is very inadequate. In the context of the work of the Health Directorate on a peer review of biomedical imbalances and treatment in the autism spectrum diagnosis, several have pointed out that the patient population often has major underlying medical problems in the digestive system and also in the immune and nervous system. These underlying medical conditions have a negative influence on development and may cause damage and secondary effects in autism, such as sensory disturbances, impaired coordination, reduced cognitive behaviour, mood swings, exposed general health, sleep problems and impaired gastrointestinal function. Parents of children with autism report significant reductions in autistic traits in children due to biomedical treatment, including: through diet, food security and medication. It contributes to better learning opportunities and significantly better quality of life for both children and families. It is also necessary to ensure equal rights with regard to reimbursement of high costs associated with the disease. I have previously addressed this in a question-time question to Councillor Bjarne Håkon Hanssen when he had responsibility for reimbursement through Nav. However, public health services are perceived to be inadequate in this area, including: because of limited knowledge of healthcare. It is important to contribute to increased knowledge of biomedical treatment and to ensure that children with autism receive a good offer of investigation and treatment. That is why the Right Party and the Advance Party have supported these concrete proposals, which we believe will contribute both to increased competence and a much better offer to these children. We have made both children and parents who show quite clear, great improvements. Then this is an important area to look at and organise so that everyone is given equal treatment throughout the country. This must not only be reserved for a small group, but made available to everyone who needs it, even though they do not have parents who are as resourceful as the ones we've met here. I would then like to present the proposal on behalf of the Right and the Advance Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4553	As the rapporteur, I would like to say that I am very pleased that the committee is behind many of the comments in the setting of the library grossing-up message. There have also been very many polite debates around the country, and also here in Oslo, so I think it has been very nice to be the case rapporteur for this message. Although the representative of the Left has stated that the message has not been worth waiting for, the Committee, in a joint note, is pleased that the Government has provided a big message on libraries covering the entire breadth and diversity of the sector. The message provides a good overview of the library area and provides the basis for further development and strengthening of the various libraries. The message is used to develop adaptive libraries that can provide better library services. In this context, libraries need to adapt their services to users and develop skills and new forms of communication. The user interface is central to the development of the libraries. The aim of the message is to provide the basis for a government policy that can help libraries meet future challenges in a digital time and in a multicultural society. Libraries are part of knowledge and culture policy, and libraries are important meeting places in the multicultural community. The journalist and author Mustafa Can, who is a Kurdish immigrant in Sweden, writes in his book Tett until the days: «The library was not just walls with books in beautiful rows; it was a universe and a variety of horizons that took me out on a journey, beyond the narrow limits of the city of the killer... I owned the world by having access to the library.» I think this is a beautiful description of how important libraries are from an integration perspective. The People's Library is the main and most visited cultural arena in the municipality. Libraries should help to strengthen democracy and freedom of expression, and the message states that the public libraries should remain free in all municipalities, something that SV is very pleased about. A free library offer gives everyone the opportunity to participate and have access to books, networks and journals, regardless of background and economy. A good library offer will help build up class barriers, and it is a good learning venue and a democracy builder. The digital challenges will shape the development of libraries in the future. Digitalisation and dissemination of digital material face copyright challenges in libraries, and it is therefore important that the sector strengthens its competence in this area. Licensed materials are very important sources of knowledge in libraries. Licences must be negotiated as a national asset, where public libraries can also provide such access. In its note, the Government asks the Ministry of Culture and the Church and the Ministry of Knowledge to continue to work on the scope, organisation and financing of such national licences. I would also like to stress the importance of the provision of up-to-date and accessible university and higher education libraries with regard to licensed material. Computer games are now an important part of the culture of children and young people. Libraries should contribute to creating social and secure frameworks around computer games while giving this cultural expression a legitimate place. I want to highlight Norwegian computer games – they are fun and of high quality, and they should be an offer at our libraries in the future. Knowledge of computer games must also be included as part of the necessary enhancement of the competence of the library sector. The entire Committee welcomes the fact that the message provides for closer cooperation between different libraries, while at the same time opening up to the development of different cooperation models. Incentives for cooperation and coordination can help municipalities to test different models. Competence development programme is a necessary effort to strengthen libraries. I have visited many libraries before the processing of the message, and I would say that many places around the country are now making exciting advances to become model libraries. I've been to Molde, which is a place where you've really worked with this kind of thinking in the future. The Ministry also wants to promote a case on changes to the Library Act. It's necessary and good. There is a strong need for overall national policies in key areas of library competence development and service development. It is good that the management of musical instruments for cultural buildings is transferred to the county. This means that investment aid for libraries and mobile services is part of this scheme. A national literature actor is now appointed in the cultural school system. Norwegian Authorcentrum has extensive experience with authoring tours and good regional coverage. This is positive. As a national author of literature in the cultural school system, the Norwegian Author Centre must contribute to the dissemination of literature productions based on professional literature, foreign literature and literature by deceased authors to students. We will underline the library's task as the learning venue, and especially the important role of the school libraries, and are pleased that the Government has launched a separate school library development programme. This will contribute to the professional strengthening of the school libraries, which is particularly important and necessary. Government parties believe it is important that scientific literature and prose are communicated in the same way as the other literature, which is covered by state cultural policy through the purchasing systems, for example. snow literature. We are pleased that the Department assesses an appropriate way of strengthening the dissemination of scientific literature and of case law in the libraries. We are pleased with the increased investment in the prison libraries that have been implemented in recent years. Government parties note that the message provides the basis for the patient libraries to be administratively anchored in the healthcare system. It is important that this conversion ensures patients' right to library services, so that this service is not impaired. The Committee welcomes the strengthening of the Norwegian Audio and Braille Library and is aware that the KABB Audio and Blind Library provides a very important and complementary offer for the blind and partially syndicated. In addition to the NLB offer, KABB’s publications are a natural part of the overall national supply of library services to the blind and partially sighted. Children, young people and adults who are completely or partly speechless need alternative forms of communication to express themselves and understand. For many of these, alternative or supplementary communication, ASK, is the only option. The Committee calls for an assessment of the inclusion of an offer to this group as part of a national library offer. It is necessary to strengthen libraries as contributors to inclusion, integration and cultural diversity. For this reason, it is important for all groups that have different problems making their literature available, that there is also an offer in the libraries. The multilingual library makes important contributions to the entire sector in this area. Through this service, all libraries can provide better services to their users. Today, we are also addressing the message on national strategy for the digital preservation and dissemination of cultural heritage. This message is closely associated with the library messages, and updates that we need a level of competence in the sector. ICT policy in the field of culture is to maximise the availability of our collections in archives, libraries and museums for the most possible future use of ICT solutions. The collections shall be searchable and accessible across the entire ABM field, and the content shall be communicated user-oriented, so that it is easily accessible. It's a raging development in this area that we can only understand the consequences of. Social media is the everyday life of children and young people. It is forward-looking and exciting, and it offers unparalleled opportunities for a more fair and democratic information and knowledge society. Finally, I would like to say that some of the criticisms that have come against the library message are that there is a lot to consider and assume, etc., but it is clear that we are going to follow up what is in the message. Any communication in the field of culture addressed by this government has been followed up by budgetary funds. We're going to have a cultural promise in the future, as this government has done. There is a historical commitment to culture, and it is to be continued, so that these two messages are also followed up with annual appropriations, but it depends on a new red-green government.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4600	The new ICT technology opens up entirely new opportunities both to preserve our cultural heritage and to make it available and completely different opportunities to produce different types of source materials across traditional sector-wide display sites. The vision of the Government's ICT policy in the field of culture is to make as much of our collections in archives, libraries and museums as possible available. The purpose of the Communication is to launch a strategy to achieve these objectives. Access to ICT in a rapidly changing society is an instrument to maintain and further develop fundamental democratic values such as: was the basis for public information and the making available of libraries. Wide public information was a prerequisite for democratic development, where a knowledge-based basis for participation in society was created. The library message carries this vision, but is based on how a forward-looking library must be more than a book collection where you can get knowledge. Library in our global information society looks completely different. It shall facilitate information as well as the experience of art and culture — based on physical meeting places, but also on virtual experiences and meeting places. It shall provide for individual immersion and opportunities for shared discussions and experiences. The digital world will provide completely new opportunities and new requirements for the design and competence of a modern library and the services to be provided. The core is that the new opportunities offered by the ICT community should be made available, provide users with new opportunities to experience and to participate actively in society. At the heart of the development of libraries is the work of digitising our cultural heritage. Digitisation of cultural heritage can contribute to a more fair and democratic information and knowledge society. The government sees access to cultural heritage as a central step towards the realisation of the cultural digital public. I am pleased to say that there is a broad consensus on the assessments made and the measures proposed in the Communication, although some shades are present. The work on digitisation takes place in several areas, in different sectors, centrally, regionally and locally. In this context, a number of instruments need to be coordinated. The Committee supports the implementation of actions aimed at joint solutions for the digital preservation, making available and dissemination of their collections by sectors and institutions. It is the Swedish Archives Agency and the National Library together with ABM development and the museums that form the foundation of a national digitisation initiative in the area of the Ministry of Culture and the Church. The majority of the Committee subscribes to the unique operational sector responsibilities underpinning the division of labour. The National Library is the main player in the digitisation work in the library field and has sole responsibility for national bibliographical standards and digital solutions for searching across all libraries. The Archives Agency shall have equivalent operational responsibility for the archive side, while ABM development shall have coordinating and facilitating tasks for museums and private archives outside the Archives Agency, but no direct operational tasks in the digitisation context. The Committee stresses the importance of establishing a comprehensive overview of the contents and services of Norwegian libraries, archives and museums. In this context, national standards for digitisation in the ABM field are important. National joint solutions for archive, library and museum data will form the basis for an integrated search that generates cross-institutional responses and points directly to content from different bases and sources. As well as the law on copyright and the law on disclosure of personal data, the law on copyright and the law on disclosure of personal data provides a framework for digital making available. The same is true of the administrative provisions on professional secrecy. The relationship between society's desire for access to protected material and the rights of authors is a core issue in terms of digital accessibility. The Committee is aware that this is also among the themes addressed in the revision of the copyright law. The Committee welcomes the cooperation of the National Library with Norwegian copyright organisations to make Norwegian works digitally available over the Internet. The National Library's pilot project Boekylla.no may in the long term form a model for simplified clearance through the contract licence under the copyright law § 16a. This project may form a model for arrangements with contract licences also for other copyrighted material.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4634	We in the Progress Party are pleased that there is now a big message about library and a digitalisation of our cultural heritage. The Progress Party believes that the Norwegian cultural heritage represents great values for Norwegian society. It is our common history and memory and is central to our modern society. We see archives, libraries and museums as central to the preservation of our cultural heritage and its dissemination in today's societies and to future generations. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4744	This government has provided many messages during this magnification period, including in the cultural field. I think we've now reached the 17th big message that we're dealing with from the department, and that means that we're about to go through all the different arts and cultural fields. There's still a big message about museums, coming to summer, but then we've basically got a complete review of all the sectors. It may be difficult to rank the different messages. I have previously stated that the language message is probably the most important cultural message of this period, but I would say that these twin messages, the library message and the digitisation message are closely behind the language message. The Digitalisation Communication is an offensive national strategy for the realisation of an Internet-based culture and knowledge-based public opinion for the benefit and joy of the entire population. I don't think it's too strongly said to say that the technological change we're now undergoing in digitalization can only be compared to the huge change we got when printing was invented. The way we can now store, disseminate and use information by making practical, gradually, all of humanity's accumulated knowledge available at a key pressure will change the way society looks, and it challenges us in how to achieve the ordinary, important, political goals of justice, an opportunity for everyone, participation and democracy in a digital age. Often there is attention to the problems, at the latest today we heard that the music industry threatened with a report, I think they went to court, against Telenor because of the illegal distribution of material, for which the rightholders were then not paid. We'll experience the same thing with movies, as the bandwidth increases, and as the user interface and readability improve, we'll probably experience the same thing in books. But in the midst of all these challenges of payment flows and copyright protection, we should not forget the amazing tool of digitisation can be, and what incredible opportunities it offers. We have huge collections in Norway of books, objects, different things that are an important part of our cultural heritage. Our ambition is to make as many of these collections available as possible through digitisation. It will provide us with new opportunities for the dissemination of our culture, both through museums and libraries and ordinary channels, as well as in other areas of society, such as education, research and other societal goals. The challenge of digital preservation, making available and disseminating the collections in the ABM field is many, both legal, collection and organisational. First of all, the job itself is enormous. It's only for 10 to 15 years -- let's say 15, for a little bit -- that we've lived in the digital age. Everything before it has to be reviewed and digitised for the first time. And it goes without saying that most of what humanity has produced from material was produced before the mid-1990s. So it's a huge problem we have to do. Fortunately, technology helps us. It's getting easier and faster to scan things and put things out, but it's a formidable work we're going to do. Then it's important that we have good priorities. What is important to digitize first, and how much should be digitized? This must be done after professional assessments in the institutions, but there must also be a social perspective on it: how should we ensure that the most important thing is made available first? Then we have to ask: how can we get the central actors across the entire archive, library and museum field to implement a comprehensive digital collection management, how can we make this uniquely simple both for users and for those who digitise, how can we get a clear operational sector responsibility in digitisation, and who should be responsible for which sectors? We give an answer to this in the message. The Archives and the National Library shall develop long-term storage systems which shall also cover the collections of the Museums, and we shall also develop sector-specific search services and joint searches across institutional boundaries and collections. The goal is that this is not just digitized, but that it becomes simple, unbureaucratic and understandable for people most to use these collections. We're already making a lot of material available. Bookhylla.no, which was recently launched, is publishing part of our literature heritage. But we also try out new and innovative digital communication services within the ABM field. The library message takes care of the entire library sector, but has a special look at the public libraries. Some have asked themselves if we need the library longer in the middle of this digital age, where we can eventually reach the entire world library and world information with our computer from home. The answer to the big message is clear: Yes, we need the library. People's libraries should still be a place where you can find the information you need, where you can find the information you might not know that you needed, especially in the field of culture, but also other types of information, where you can find resources, information, knowledge and incentives that are not initially easy to find, and not least where you get professional assistance from people who know how to find out in the information society. The objective we have that everyone should have the opportunity to participate, that everyone should have the opportunity to acquire knowledge, that everyone should have the opportunity to use the library as a resource in their education, in their development and in their formation, is equally important, if not more importantly, in a society where the digital class divide or the information divide may be at risk of increasing. The library shall also be a meeting place, not just a resource. I think we have to turn around a little bit the way we look at the library, because some of us have grown up with the library primarily like a giant bookcase, a very large room of newspaper clips and books and some nice people who have knowledge to help us out. I think that developing the library like the learning arena, a place where people meet, where cultural activities take place, a generalisation where we actually have interpersonal contact, is an important future development for the libraries. We also write about this in the library message: One of the objectives is to develop robust and resilient libraries that provide users with good library services and a library worth using in the community. It requires more interaction between the public libraries locally and regionally, more cooperation between different types of libraries, such as: operation, dissemination, technological infrastructure and empowerment. We are not in favour of a coercion of the smallest libraries, although we see that there is a precarious lack of professionally trained staff in the smallest municipalities. Instead, we want to use positive instruments, giving extra resources and incentives to those who make good cooperation projects. We want voluntary cooperation agreements between the state and the county councils on library development. We want to develop model libraries that promise good, forward-looking libraries. We want to strengthen our competence, and we also clarify the responsibilities between ABM development and the National Library in the library field. I think this is a proper strategy. If we demand that others use the resources efficiently, well and appropriately, we must also look at the state instruments. Digital development is a major challenge for libraries. Today's tool for the library, such as purchasing systems, etc., need to be adapted to digital developments, such as: with national licences, so that we can coordinate the resources and facilitate the sharing of libraries around the country on things that we have jointly cleared. In the library message it is proposed that 2010 be a national academic year. It will be a good opportunity both to lift the libraries and to lift the important task of promoting literacy and literacy. I am proud of these two messages. I think they're pointing far ahead. I am also pleased that there is such broad support for them in the committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4916	In St. prp. No 65 The Government proposes a simplification and conversion of the Norwegian Cultural Council’s appointment and composition legislation. The conclusions of the Ministry are based on the final report of the Løken Committee, simplified, coordinated and independent, issued on 5 June 2008, including: was mandated to look at various aspects of the composition, operation and functioning of the Norwegian Cultural Council. The present grossing-up proposal concerns only the rules on the appointment and composition of the Council of Culture and not the other aspects of the Council's activities. The majority agree with the Ministry’s reasoning that a council of fewer members can work more dynamically and efficiently than a council of 13 members, the current number. The Ministry proposes ten members, a slightly higher number than recommended in the Løken report. The majority of the Committee agrees that this could be an appropriate size of the Council. We also agree that the council as a whole is appointed by the King on a proposal from the Ministry of Culture and the Church. It could contribute to overall thinking around the composition of the Council, so that this would be a well-functioning college. The Government proposes that artistic and cultural competence should be the applicable criteria for the appointment of the Board of Governors. This does not mean, of course, that other important experience from, for example: other policies, organisational work or social life are uninterested in the work of the Council. However, the majority agree that Council members should not represent specific parties or organisations, as has been the case in the past. By emphasising the artistic and cultural background of members in the appointment process, the Norwegian Culture Council will be able to provide important complementary competences in relation to state, regional and local cultural governance. The majority fraction in the Committee has highlighted in particular the importance of the broad geographical representation of the Council. There are increasing numbers of professional freelancers who want to live in different regions of the country, and it is of great importance that the members of the Norwegian Cultural Council have good dialogue with and broad knowledge of these cultural environments. This is a price tip which should also apply to the composition of the various committees of the Council of Culture. It is worth noting that most of the opinions of the Løken Committee’s report, which refers to the Norwegian Cultural Council, are positive that the Council must be broadly composed and agree in principle that artistic and cultural criteria must form the basis for the appointment of Council members. Some of the comments the opposition makes in this matter are, in fact, a little bit difficult to understand. It is not obvious that government-appointed members of the Council will have closer political ties than members appointed by the magnification, which the opposition parties seem to believe – especially when the members of the Council from now on are actually to be appointed on an art and cultural basis, and not, as in the past, on the basis of party political or organisational affiliation. Nor can I see that the opposition in any way suggests how they believe that the council can best be appointed. The opposition also claims that it is impossible to address the issue of appointment and composition without involving all aspects of the functioning of the Council of Culture. I disagree. It is fully possible to define specific issues relating to the appointment of the Council and compositional criteria — and then, at a later stage, to address different other aspects of the Council's activities. I am pleased with the proposals for amendments for the appointment of the Norwegian Cultural Council submitted by the Ministry. I believe that both the Norwegian Cultural Council and cultural life will benefit from it.

It would have been better if the government by this crossroads had realized that this would be hanging in the air, and admitted that it in this area did not come into being satisfactorily. In order to decide on this matter, the Stortinget must be invited to a wide-ranging discussion of the tasks of the Norwegian Cultural Council and the location in the instrument. The right-wing party therefore believes that the matter should be sent back to the Government. From the point of view of the Right, this matter appears to be another move to concentrate power on the ministry. The geographical complexity of the Council does not prevent this. The competences desired by the Council are closely related to the actual tasks of the Council and the location of the Board of Governors in the instrument. Is it really the case that professional considerations such as the decentralisation or the preservation of the culture of the people should no longer be present? The question goes to the Center Party. And of course, it's not that only the department can find professionally qualified people. It's a very good thing that other agencies can do that. There's a question you can put to the Storting too. We have heard throughout this period the honeycomb term 'arm length distance' as the answer to our reflection of power-centering action by the Government. In the case of Norwegian Cultural Councils, the power perspective should be raised and the arm's length distance concept further analysed. How long is the road really going to be? And what consideration is given to cultural life in the light of the government's allocation of money and the criteria for which they are granted? It has been said that the new members should be better trained professionally and give the Council greater integrity in this respect. But what does that really mean in practical life? If this is to have some substance, it must also mean that the members of the Council should have a more extensive task and go deeper into the proceedings. And what will the relationship between advice and selection be? It is difficult to see that this does not mean a shift of power from a selection of professionals to advice – and therefore also closer to the department. Today, too, we have good reason to believe that there is some sort of hint from the Ministry of Culture to the Council of Culture about the desired priorities. In short, here are the questions in the queue. We have got a narrow tile of a case about the management of the most important agency in the instrument across the entire free field within Norwegian cultural life. This is short and good for thin. It is not that I am primarily concerned that the Workers' Party – despite good traditions – should appoint party traps or people loyal to the party as members of the Cultural Council. That's not the point of power concentration. The point is that in one case after another, we see that the department becomes the appointing authority, and so we recruit board members – or council members in this context – based on one environment and what one environment places on criteria. I think that over time is an unfortunate development that should be actively countered. Of course, it is possible to decide on this matter, as the representative Knutsen did. It's just that I don't think it's appropriate. It's far from reasonable to do that. So I'm dealing with what the opposition has gathered about by proposals, which are about to send the matter back to the government, let the council sit on, and that we get a real deal about this. It is not a big problem to continue the way you have appointed the Cultural Council so far. The Cultural Council is working very well, as far as I can see.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4957	The representative, Olemic Thommess, has addressed the proposals to which he referred.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4960	Norwegian Cultural Council is an important institution. The Council manages resources in a way that contributes to the creation of growth and development in Norwegian cultural life. Now the state councils are tightening their grip on Norwegian cultural councils. Knut Olav Åmås said it like this in the Evening Post on May 9th this year: ‘Gisske’s grip on Norwegian Culture Council is an answer to no questions asked, a solution to no one’s problems.’ That might have been well said. The main content of the Government's proposal is to shift power to the political leadership of the Ministry of Culture at all times. I believe it is a democratic strength that the elected assembly contributes to the composition of the Council of Culture. This is a democratic function we will lose with the Government's proposals. It has always been important to protect schemes that ensure an arm's length distance between the Council of Culture and the Ministry. With the Government's proposals, the Council of Culture becomes to a greater extent a governing body under the Ministry. It limits the political space of the Council of Culture and weakens the role of the Council as a cultural policy actor. I think it's unfortunate. We need a cultural council that has a certain space of action, and that can fill the role of corrective and developmentist – with a certain distance from government and greatness. Under the red-green government, cultural policy has become increasingly streamlined. Some cleanups have been positive, and we have supported them. But in the eagerness to clean up, the instrument must not be streamlined to such an extent that the flexibility, diversity and critical voices disappear. That's what's about to happen. It is important that artistic and cultural judgement is spread among several actors. The dissemination of power is necessary to strengthen the full breadth of Norwegian cultural life. If the Government appoints the entire Board of Governors, the spread of power is further weakened. The pattern we've seen over time from the Government. The independent position of the Culture Council is weakened. The management of budget proposals is more detailed than under previous governments, and the Culture Council is no longer involved in the main distribution of funds. The Christian People's Party wants a free and independent cultural life. The development we are now seeing, where more and more power is centralized to the ministry, is worrying. And we have to be especially vigilant in times when state grants are increasing. I have also noticed that many of the hearing bodies that gave their opinion on the report of the Løken Committee have pointed to this and are concerned about what is now happening. The Government proposes to reduce the number of members of the Board of Governors. But the Council of Culture has its value in ensuring diversity and breadth. Then the Council must be able, and have resources, to take care of the full breadth of art and culture. The Council shall capture new impulses from all over the country. Then there is no reason to do this slimming. A wide-ranging Council, with a sufficient number of members, is a strength, not a weakness. It is also important that the Council is given the opportunity to be professionally up-to-date and also to experience a part of the cultural life for which they will be involved and to consider grants. I hope that the Council of State will include it in the further assessment of that work.

But the core we're going to reveal. The core is to gather as much power as possible in one place. It is not clear from the matter why it is so important to do this, or why it is so urgent. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e4998	The Government proposes changes to the rules on the appointment and composition of the Norwegian Cultural Council on the basis of a selection set up by the Ministry, the Løken Committee, which reviewed the arrangements for the award of State grants for cultural purposes. They proposed a number of changes, which are also relevant to the Council of Culture, including: changes in the rules governing the appointment and composition of the Board of Governors. The Løken Committee proposes that the members of the Council as a whole be appointed by the Ministry of Culture and the Church, possibly by the King, on a proposal from the Department, and that they be appointed on the basis of artistic and cultural criteria. They also propose that the number of members should be reduced and that the appointments should be rolling. Today, as you know, the Grand Duchy appoints four members and the King nine members, two of whom, on a proposal from the CS. In the hearings, this proposal received broad support from those who expressed their views on it. There is therefore no reason to conclude otherwise than that cultural life itself wants this change to be welcomed. The fact that the opposition in the Storting has a different view is, of course, only to take note of it, but it is not in that view that there is a particular representativeness in terms of what cultural life wants. If we see developments in a historical perspective, we see that the Council of Culture has changed its position from the mid-1960s, when it was established, until today. In 1965 there was no separate Ministry of Culture. The various cultural institutions were few and had few employees. Later we got our own Ministry of Culture, and we've got strong cultural institutions, with big trade unions. Municipalities and county councils have also had a strong development of cultural competence. The Cultural Council has become more a body of division on the basis of artistic judgement. In 1965, the Culture Council distributed a quarter of the entire cultural budget. Today, the percentage is approx. 7 – as far as I remember. The members of the Council should be independent and not represent organisations, institutions or parties. They shall work on the principle of arm’s length distance from both political authorities, from the market and from different interests. The Council should have full autonomy in its artistic discretionary exercise, and it should be the artistic and cultural discretion on which it is based when it comes to asking for the qualities and characteristics of its members. To date, the Council has been appointed by various actors. I would have had nothing against finding a solution that could have continued this, if we could still have ensured that it was the artistic and cultural judgements and the artistic and cultural qualities that would underlie the appointments. However, we have seen that excellent people from the great parties – I have also been involved in this – have entered the Cultural Council, perhaps not primarily from an artistic and cultural background, but more from a cultural policy point of view. Our idea is that we need to have a clearer line between who's going to be involved in cultural policy and who's going to be involved with the artistic discretion. Cultural policy shall be exercised by those who are either elected or who are elected by the people – and who can thus be cast by the people. This room shall conduct cultural policy. The Culture Committee in Stortinget shall conduct cultural policy. The Ministry of Culture must, of course, pursue cultural policy. The Culture Council will provide good cultural policy advice, but will primarily carry out an art and cultural assessment work – at arm's length away from us politicians. It is very difficult to find the red – or possibly the blue or the dark blue – thread in the opposition's policy. When we proposed that the Stortinget instead of the King in the Council of State should decide on the cultural policy priorities around the Council of Culture, the opposition said no. In the setting it is stated in a note from the opposition – Right, Christian People’s Party and Left – that ‘Norwegian cultural councils are no longer invited to participate in the main distribution of funds’. This is wrong. The Council is allowed to make a major request on how they want the priorities to be achieved. We have even introduced a meeting every year in advance of the budget, a meeting that the previous government did not like to implement. It also states that: «the entries in the budget proposals are more detailed than those previously on which they were based». That's right, but these detailed leaders were formerly with the King in the State Council – not in the Storting. So it's a democratisation – a more open process and a greater transparency – that is now underpinned. We support the settings of the Løken selection. But we've made some changes. We believe that the Council should be larger, in order to get a greater breadth, and that it is the King of the Council of State and not the Ministry who should appoint it, so that we also get a bigger process here, with more departments. We are pleased that the Storing joins these changes.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e5382	As the rapporteur, I would like to take the majority position that all the parties, except the Advance Party, are behind. I would like to start by pointing out that this matter was raised at the end of the spring session, and that two of the parties in the Committee, the Party of Advancement and the Right, in a minutes meeting of the Transport Committee on 8 June, as set out in the case, refuse to deal with several cases due to a short period of time. In the Ministry of Justice and Police's answer to the questions raised by the members of the Progressive Party in the Committee, the background for the short term is affected. I would like to stress that the majority of the Committee has shown great goodwill in order to have the matter dealt with. So to the matter itself. In December 2004, the Storting decided that the Ministry of Justice and Police could conclude contracts in 2005 for the first extension of a common digital radio connection for emergency and emergency services in the police districts of Follo, Rome, Oslo, Asker and Bærum, Søndre Buskerud and Østfold. In the treatment of St.prp. No 30 for 2006-2007, the Stortinget decided to launch a first step in the development of a new digital emergency network in Norway, and the Ministry of Justice and Police was authorised to conclude an extension contract in the rest of the country, subject to the condition that nationwide deployment cannot be started until the first stage evaluation has been completed and the Stortinget may have adopted further development. In addition to these cases, the size has been informed about the status and progress and the handling of emergency networks in several cases during the period. The issue we are going to discuss today is the proposal that Stortinget authorises the King to increase the cost framework for the first stage of construction of the emergency network by up to 110 million. kr in relation to the current cost framework for the first stage of construction and the total framework. The negotiated cost framework for a two-step nationwide deployment of emergency networks was in St.prp. No 1 Appendix No 3 for 2004–2005 estimated at DKK 3.6 billion. The reasons why there may be a need to increase the framework are: the contractor performing the contract, Nokia Siemens Networks Norge AS, has been further delayed in relation to the previously notified sites has revealed the need to modify specifications that there is an increased resource need in DNK for the follow-up of the supplier there are increased costs associated with the deployment of the sites there is a potential need for increased capacity in the network and potential needs related to enhanced coverage of special features beyond what is specified in the contract there are currency fluctuations; The Committee has been notified of delays in the project in several cases from 2006 until now. Based on the current progress plan, it is now expected that the police will deploy the emergency network in operational use from no earlier than the fourth quarter of 2009. The majority of the Committee will express strong concern that the emergency network cannot be put into service as planned. Delays are very unfortunate for society and emergency situations, which are in great need of the new digital radio link to replace the existing analogue radio networks. The floors have now waited a very long time for a new emergency network. There has not been a particular investment in the updating of existing networks at this time. The majority of the Committee therefore considers it important that the first stage of development is now completed and put into operation within the current progress and cost plan. The DNK is responsible for the deployment and management of the emergency network. They shall ensure that binding progress plans are followed up by the supplier so that emergency budgets can plan to carry out their tasks as far as possible in line with assumptions and carry out ongoing assessments of the cost developments of the project. We assume that this work will be followed up further, and that the Ministry of Justice and Police will put in place any necessary measures to complete stage 1 of development within the current progress plans and cost frameworks. The Committee considers it important to remain informed about the development of the project, and therefore calls on the majority to provide a state-of-the-art state with a progress plan and a cost overview for stage 1 of development in the context of the submission of the State budget for 2010. Finally, I would like to stress that it is important that an emergency network be set up to provide society, the public and the public with the necessary security so that the emergency and emergency missions can be properly managed, and that the emergency network operates according to their intentions. The size has therefore decided to carry out an evaluation of the first stage of development before any decision on nationwide development is taken. It is described in the case that some evaluation activities have already been carried out and started. It is assumed that the evaluation will be carried out as foreseen and submitted for examination before any further development is adopted. I also refer to the setting and the committee's recommendation.

I think that when you still don't have it in place, and there's still a lot of uncertainty, it's sad that the majority of people don't think it makes sense for the Storting to be properly oriented along the way, so that you can be sure that the progress you're always hearing is on the run, is actually on the run as well, and that you get the emergency network in place during 2009. I'm afraid you're a long way out in 2010 before you really get it in place. As I said, it's an incredibly important tool that those who work in the rescue settings have to put in place, so that they get the emergency network they've been promised for a long time, that it actually comes in place so that they can start using it. I would like to refer to the proposal of the Advance Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-16.ana.ud2010e5464	Let me first say that I understand the representative Hoksrud well. This isn't a lucky way to deal with so extensive issues, and if we had the information before, we would have come to the Stortinget earlier, or if the Storing Session had been longer, which I think the few people want, we would have had better time. So there's been unfortunate circumstances around this. There is no wish from the Government to have such a short procedure in Stortinget. The more important it is, of course, that we are given the full opportunity to come back to this matter in the State budget. It is clear that we have put our heads on the block if we have provided information that would prove to be inaccurate, so here the whole Stortinget will have the full opportunity to go closer to the various elements when the matter is dealt with on later occasions. It's in St.prp. No 67 for 2008-2009, the status of the deployment of the emergency network. It has now become clear that there are further challenges and delays to the project. The Ministry became aware of the need for an increased cost framework at such a late stage that it was not possible to propose a power of attorney to increase the cost framework at an earlier date. It would have been natural to revise it, but then we had not had enough knowledge and had not been given the quality assurance of the information we had. The most important thing is that Stortinget is still behind us getting a new emergency network. It shall replace the existing analogue radio network and contribute to the smooth functioning of emergency and emergency seats. This is a big investment. It's a huge effort to make sure that our emergency situations can do a good job. The State concluded a fixed price contract with Siemens AS. The contract shall be conducted by Nokia Siemens Networks Norway AS. The supplier shall carry out the planning and deployment of the radio network, provide equipment for fire, police and health communication centres and radio terminals, and further ensure that all technical components operate separately and collectively, build up and provide continuous operator services over the lifetime of the emergency network, provide training and manage the implementation of the system in the three floors. An evaluation of the first stage relating to contractual tests, technology, utility and use, economy and project implementation shall be carried out before the Government submits, where appropriate, proposals to the Stortinget for decisions on further development. The Ministry was authorised to conclude a contract for the development of the first stage with a cost framework of 900 million. kr. Most of the radio network in the first stage is built. In Eastfold and Follo police districts, where the radio network is planned to be used first, 81 out of 84 stations are built. There is testing of all functions, and whether the coverage in the area is good enough. The police have used nine of their ten communication centres against the regular telephone network and the old radio system with good results. Several fire and health communication centres are expected to be delivered during 2009 and approximately 5 300 radio terminals have been delivered. The challenges and delays of the project are now particularly related to uncertainty and progress in the development of fire and health communication centres. It is still expected that the radio network will be completed during 2009 and that the police will take emergency networks into operational use from no earlier than the fourth quarter of 2009. The delays have led to the need for the State to maintain its project organisation for the follow-up of the supplier longer than planned and with major resource efforts. In addition to the supplier’s delays in the deployment and commissioning of the radio network, challenges have been attached to the development and delivery of all the agreed functions contained in the contract. In addition, changes and additions to the specifications of the health and fire communication centres have been identified. These challenges cannot be resolved within the current cost framework of the project and the changes and additions are deemed necessary to enable the network to be put into operation. The delay of the supplier and the need for change of the emergency budgets may result in higher costs than previously foreseen, and it is therefore likely that the first stage of development will not be completed within the current cost framework and that a mandate was therefore needed to increase the cost framework by the various underlying conditions: changing specifications increased resource needs of the directorate increased costs of the networks possible need for increased capacity of the network possible needs related to enhanced coverage of special features, and exchange rate fluctuations Based on our calculations, an overall additional demand of up to 110 million is indicated. kr. The final need will be assessed and clarified during the summer of 2009, following negotiations with the supplier by the Emergency Communications Directorate. Any increase in costs is not expected to result in the need for increased appropriations in 2009 since the delay has led to the non-fulfilment of the framework already allocated to the budget. Of course, we come back with the matter in a more thorough explanation of what happened during the summer when the budget was processed in the autumn.
